Until 1978, the boron mines, run by the hands of foreign and domestic private companies, were handed over to Etibank, by Law 2172. In 1983, Law 2840 ruled that the search and operation of boron salts would be carried out by the state. The Boron mines were adopted under Mining Law 3213, which was issued in 1985, but the laws of Law 2840 were kept secret. The Bor Salts, signed by the Ministry of Energy and Natural Resources and signed by the Council of Ministers, were sent to the chairman of the Turkish Parliament on March 20, 2012, to conduct nuclear energy raw materials and to change the Law on the Responsibility of Liniyet and some of the Iron Fields. "For technical, commercial and economic reasons, the ownership of the product can be seen by third parties under the provisions of Public Requital Law 4734, under which the property of the product is registered. However, in the event of more than three years of tender time for the third party, demands on the issue are decided by the High Planning Board." It's called. This change means privatisation. In our country, there are still 30 productions of mines, and after years of privatisation in the mining sector, only the government has a license for boron, thorium, uranium, and coal mines. It's run by the private sector in many ways of renting coal fields. A state-owned company, such as Etibank, was taken away from its businesses -- chrome, copper, silver, aluminum, and so on -- is limited only to borron management. These mines are not produced today in thorium and uranium fields, but plans, projects, and studies continue. As a result of privatisations in the mining sector so far, public enterprises and public services have been seized, productions have fallen, employment has been severely tightened. The sector's GDP has been cut down. In the hands of the capital holders of the effort to make maximum profit in a short period of time, the production of work accidents increased dramatically, while many mining engineers and hundreds of mine workers lost their lives, without producing thousands of tons of underground resources. Despite these negative experiences, the privatisation of the government's remaining coals and boron mines has made it clear that there is no lesson being learned from the wrongs done. Bor is a strategic mine. Borial minerals have extremely specific chemical structures. There are more than 250 uses in the form of raw materials, refined products and the final product, most of which without alternatives. Borial minerals are the salt of the industry by increasing the value of the additives. Therefore, it would be appropriate to establish a parliamentary research commission in our country under Article 104 and 105 of the Constitution's 98th and TBM Institution, in order to investigate the problems of mining and the importance of the boron mines and take the necessary precautions. Nevzat Don't Fear [[Isparta]] [[Antalya]] [[İstanbul]] [[Kütahya]] [[Adana]] [[Adana]] [[Slap]] [[Elizipt]] [[Manisa]] Violence against children in our country has begun to grow intensively in all areas of society. This growing violence in society affects children physically, mentally, and emotionally. Violence, which has left a very difficult, negative, and lasting effect on children, is causing the society to suffer tremendous losses socially, economically and culturally. Their violence distances children from educational opportunities and leads to many dangerous situations, including various crimes. Many studies in our country show widespread domestic violence against children. In 2010, a total of 988 university students, 64 per cent male and 41 per cent female, reported violence in their childhood, including kicking, punching, dropping, burning, tooth loss, bone breaking. This violence was carried out by brothers and other members of the household, especially the parents. One out of five children, 22.6% of them, have explained that they have been subjected to violence for the disciplinary facility of this violence, 15.9% to teach a lesson and 16.1% to build respect. Also, another study that UNICEF and Social Services and Child Protective Services (SHCHEK) have jointly carried out shows that 45 per cent of children living in our country between the ages of 7 and 18 are subject to physical abuse, 51 per cent to emotional abuse, and 25 per cent to various neglects. In addition, the study of domestic violence among children co-organised by the Youth Life Foundation, the International Children's Association Centre and the Children's Future Foundation has produced dramatic results. In this study, emotional and physical violence is at the head of the kinds of violence that children see inside the house. At least 67.9% of the students in the class of 6.7 and 8 surveyed from elementary schools reported that they had been subjected to domestic emotional violence at least once. 37 percent of the children have reported encountering physical violence within the house at least once. The study also revealed that in our country children are neglected inside the house. At least 25.7% of the children say they've been neglected at home at least once, but the 20.5% report that they've witnessed violence within the house at least once. As you can see from the results of the investigation, violence against children has increased in every aspect of every society in our country. Increased violence against children in society has become almost a threat to their health and development. Therefore, in our country, all forms of violence against children must be prevented in every environment. Therefore, it would be appropriate to establish a parliamentary research commission under Article 104 and 105 of the Constitution's 98th and TBM Charter to investigate the problems of violence against children and take the necessary measures. tbmm-2013-12-09sit07spe0434par0104	Yeah. Kadir Gökmen Öğüt, İstanbul MP, demanding a statement on the motion.
tbmm-2013-12-09sit07spe0437par0107	Mr. President, dear lawmakers, first of all, I would like to celebrate here the liberation of our fellow MP Mustafa Balbay. I wish you the best of our House. I hope all our friends in the House share the same joy. I'm talking about the other prisoners. Our dear friends, valued lawmakers, and one of the key elements of oral and dental health is dental technicians. This is a profession that requires expertise and experience. In addition, because it is an area of occupational illness that involves many health risks and may be fatal, it is involved in heavy and dangerous work by law. Due to the use of dangerous chemicals, heavy metal alloys during the production of dental prosthetics, dental denture labs contain health risks. In Turkey, dental prosthetic service is under contract. At first, service purchase could be provided from more than one laboratory according to the workload of institutions, while the public tender law began to be given to the company that has given the lowest price in the tender, when the issue of dental prosthetic production was implemented in 2011. The only criteria for tenders are a licensed lab. Healthy safety conditions at work, work experience, and other vital issues such as this are not questioned, the lowest bidder is accepted, so the tenders are very low, and materials are produced at low prices. Low price in material means low quality and unqualified employee in product. Because of the lack of public health care and the low cost of prices, the labs are forced to employ unqualified and uninsured employees. The lab, which has won a tender far above its capacity, is producing small labs where five to 10 people work in order to grow jobs. This practice extends daily working hours between 16 and 18 hours. According to legislation in the EU and Turkey, 23 mobile prosthetics are required in a month, while one dental technician is forced to perform 26 prosthetics a day in private labs. Another key problem, the Ministry of Health, the Ministry of Public Hospitals of Turkey, has increased the amount of work that dental dental technicians in the Ministry of Health's mouth and dental services should do daily compared to the previous amount of work. Most dental technicians, especially silicone, suffer from bellioosis, lung disease, asthma, KOAH, lung cancer; skin, eyes, hearing diseases; muscle, skeletal system diseases, nervous diseases. Across Turkey, almost 10 degrees of dental technicians have died due to siliconis, but there have been dozens of deaths unregistered. Today, according to Health Ministry data, most dental technicians suffer from siliconis. These numbers cannot be expected to reflect the truth, given that occupational hospitals have three in Turkey. Ladies and gentlemen of the law, I'd like to say a few words about oral and dental technicians. The adopted Law 6354 has been severely hit by all dental technicians. With our efforts, the current law allows those with at least a year's worth of high-school education, documenting work and procedures on oral and dental techniques in the past five years, to work under the capacity of oral and dental technicians for those who are successful in the examinations established by the Ministry of Health. Regardless of the subject, employment is no doubt essential and necessary for those who have received training. We support the government's work and its relevant laws. Since the first years of the declaration of the Republic, the foundation of health care and health care in our country today has been laid. After the declaration of the Republic, many infectious diseases have been fought, and organizations related to the fight against these diseases have been established. In 1961, Law No. 224 was established, and health clinics were established, and all health-related data quickly improved. This law has been an example of socializing health care that first began in the country, and is still a model of many countries around the world. Despite the political obstacles and footsteps set in front of him, the country's people also enjoyed socialization. Although health clinics have been completely excluded from public investments, they have been the easiest access to public health care in the urban region as well. We will close the offices. The mentality, which follows, also revealed how hypocritical policy he put into it by opening 20,000 family physicians. In our country, in September 2005, the pilot's practice of family medicine took place in Düzce province, followed by other provinces, and in December 2010, the family medicine model began in the first place in our province of 81. In its universal definition of family medicine, providing health care to individuals regardless of age, gender and disease, providing primary and continuous health care to families along with the community they live in, guiding physical, psychological and social problems, co-ordinating comprehensive health care when other areas of expertise are needed. The approach to modern health care suggests that different vocational groups with different education in health care should work together. According to this approach, different trade groups, such as physicians, nurses, midwifes, health workers, environmental health technicians, medical secretaryessssss, gather together to provide health care for a common purpose. The family medicine model has also brought a new approach to the concept of (ekip), one of the main elements of socialized health care. The new model defines a new team of family physicians and family health workers. Two health workers now perform the duties of six health workers. The basic benefit expected from piloting family medicine is that the proposed model will be tested in a specific area prior to the widespread spread of its model, and improvements will be made in line with the outputs to be obtained. The Ministry of Health has spread the practice of family medicine throughout the country without even explaining what changes it has created in primary health care and health indicators. The planning and integrated presentation of health care services with protective and developer services is a fundamental and indispensable element of the understanding of modern health care. Any one of these services has a threatening quality of public health as the opportunity arises. Family health workers, along with family health care workers, are also interested in the periodic impressions of health care groups, cancer screening, family planning and immunization, as well as medical practices such as police clinics, recordings, injections, spraying, milking, rent, electricity, fuel, telephone, internet, staffing, and paying insurance. In addition, family physicians are required to perform post-mortem shifts, judicial shifts, burial shifts, even though they are out of line of duty. So like a lot of insurance companies, like a lot of insurance companies, unfortunately, it's interesting, that pharmaceutical companies, many of the pharmaceutical factories, are now in the hands of foreign monopoly, trusts, and that's going to be a huge challenge in Turkey in the coming period. tbmm-2013-12-09sit09spe0550par0359	Mehmet Haberal, MP of Zonguldak, demanding a statement on the motion.
tbmm-2013-12-09sit09spe0555par0361	Mr. President, dear lawmakers, I've made my point on this matter in the commission. Once again, after reading these substances, I saw that the transference -- the transplants -- were not well assessed today. Where did we start, where did we come from? We performed the first transplant at the university on 3 November 1975. There was no law in our country then. But with the will and the parents' signatures, we started the first transplant. Then the real truth came out. There is no law in our country. In order for the law to happen, our society needed to be shown that the organs of the dead should be used to treat the sick. So for the first time, we brought in the first cadaver kidney from the European Transplantation Association on October 10, 1978, and showed that the organs of the dead can be used to treat the patients. Later, this continued from Europe, from America. And at that time, the Grand National Assembly of Turkey and the Senate discussed these issues for a long time, and Law 2238 was passed. I, today, thank both the highly valued members of the Turkish Grand National Assembly that day, and the members of the Senate, and the chairman of the era's Religious Affairs, the press, everyone who contributed to that bill. The law that was passed that day is still at the top of the world's most modern laws. Why? Because today, in fact, organ transplants have now reached the level of developed countries in our country. At the moment, Turkey is at the top of the line on this issue. How did this happen? One, thanks to this law. The second was on December 21, 1982, with Act 2594, which we had arranged for it to be removed by the Council at the time. The property of that law, the 11th article of Law 2238, is that among patients diagnosed with the death of the brain, if there is no one near it, the organ can be removed without consent. After that, liver transplantation, heart transplantation, and other transplantations have been established in our country. So the level our country has reached today on transplantation is really, really, at a level that we can all be proud of. Actually, it's the government's job... now, when I look at that stuff, everything is brought in and organized through the Ministry of Health. So, I'm asking, what is the location of the universities? Because there was nothing in the Ministry of Health at the time, all these procedures started at the universities. So, we make an organization that brings everything, leaves it to the Ministry of Health. So, how is the Health Department going to organize this? In my opinion, the government's job is to set up state standards, make state infrastructure, control state; otherwise the government has to clear the way for people to do these things. What's more, what we need to focus on here today is that these laws really are the world's proudest laws today. So much so that even the World Health Organization has set its criteria in 1991, demanding Law 2238 and Law 2594. These laws are so perfect. The most important thing there, even today, is the matter of organ trafficking, the most important problem of all countries. How do we resolve this organ trafficking issue? Number two: How can we increase organ donation? If we discuss these things, if we can evaluate them enough here, then, I think, we'll be able to get more chronic organ patients back to life without even a change of the law. Therefore, I hereby invite once again all valued lawmakers and our society to donate organs. [[Microphone turned off by automatic device]] Because many of our people have lost their lives with solid organs, and many of our people have lost their lives in need of solid organs. So, on this occasion, if our commission and we pioneer, then I think we'll be able to contribute a lot more to our society without the need to change these laws. Thank you all. I pay my respects.
